+(see `notes/chores-09.md > ## init-clone-refactor rebase
+landing (0.42.0-4.7)`): `main`'s `0.42.0-0..-4.6` work was
+rebased on top of the branch at its `0.41.1` close-out tip,
+so the branch's commits are now ancestors of `main`. The
+branch has since been deleted locally and remotely.
+
+The "still deferred" / "still open" wording in the dated
+sections below is superseded — the merge-direction question
+is resolved (rebase-main-onto-branch), and the chores-NN
+ordering follow-up survives only as a low-priority entry in
+`notes/todo.md > ## Todo`. This file is retained as a
+historical post-mortem; the recovery playbook and
+diagnostic techniques remain reusable.
+## Strategic decision (resolved 2026-05-11)
+The merge-direction call, once the recovered branch
+gave a clean base to choose from:
+Resolved: the "vice versa" topology — `main`'s
+`0.42.0-0..-4.6` work rebased on top of the branch at
+its `0.41.1` close-out tip, force-pushed at close-out
+(`0.42.0-4.7`). See Resolution above and
+`notes/chores-09.md > ## init-clone-refactor rebase
+landing (0.42.0-4.7)`.
+## Open questions (resolved — see Resolution above)
+All resolved by the `0.42.0-4.7` rebase landing:
+- **Merge direction** — settled: `main`'s `0.42.0-X`
+  work rebased on top of the branch at its `0.41.1`
+  close-out tip.
+- **chores-NN ordering** — survives only as a
+  low-priority entry in `notes/todo.md > ## Todo`.
+- **Conflict surface against 0.42.0** — the real
+  conflicts (`Cargo.toml`, `notes/todo.md`,
+  `notes/chores-08.md`, CLAUDE.md, `src/init.rs`,
+  `src/scope.rs`, `src/common.rs`) surfaced and were
+  resolved during the cascade; see
+  `notes/chores-09.md > ### What needed real work`.
+# por/dual parity + bidirectional conversion
+
+Forward-looking design capture (pre-design). Concrete work
+lands when a cycle picks it up — likely on top of the
+`--scope` rollout (the `0.50.0` work in `notes/todo.md`).
+
+`vc-x1` today treats the **dual** workspace (a code repo
+plus a `.claude` bot-session repo, cross-linked by `ochid:`
+trailers, configured by `.vc-config.toml`) as the primary
+shape; **por** (Plain Old Repo — a single repo, no
+`.claude/`, no `.vc-config.toml`) is supported but bolted
+on. Two goals:
+
+## 1. Parity
+
+por and dual should be first-class equals across every
+subcommand — same code paths, same quality of support, no
+"dual plus an afterthought" asymmetry. The `--scope`
+rollout (`code,bot` vs `por` — see `notes/todo.md` and
+`src/options_flags/scope.rs`'s `ScopeKind`) is the
+substrate; parity is the goal that rollout serves.
+
+## 2. Bidirectional conversion
+
+A new capability: convert a workspace between the two
+shapes in place.
+
+- **`por → dual`** — attach a `.claude` companion repo and
+  a `.vc-config.toml` to an existing single repo; emit
+  cross-links going forward.
+- **`dual → por`** — detach the `.claude` companion; drop
+  `.vc-config.toml`; the code repo continues standalone.
+
+## Open questions (resolve at design time)
+
+- Surface: a new subcommand (`vc-x1 convert`?
+  `promote` / `demote`?) vs. flags on existing commands.
+- `dual → por` — what happens to the `.claude` history:
+  archived, left in place untouched, deleted? Leading
+  guess is "left alone, just unlinked," but TBD.
+- `.vc-config.toml` — creation on `por → dual` (which
+  fields, defaults from user config?) and removal on
+  `dual → por`.
+- Whether `init` / `clone` change, or conversion is purely
+  a separate operation on an already-existing workspace.
+- Idempotency / re-running; recovery from a conversion
+  interrupted mid-way.
+
+## See also
+
+- `notes/todo.md` — the por/dual item, and the `--scope`
+  cluster it builds on.
+- `src/options_flags/scope.rs` — `ScopeKind` (`CodeBot` /
+  `Por`) and `parse_scope_kind`.
+- `notes/chores-07.md > ## init + clone redesign (0.41.1)`
+  — the `init_one` / `clone_one` primitives that aim to
+  give a single chokepoint for scope-shaped changes.

+   Pre-design; goal + open questions in the stub. [91]

No need for `# Edits` section in `chores-xx.md` that information will be in the
commit message and it should be the source of truth. Two suggestions:
1) The section title in a chores-xx.md file should exactly match the name of the commit.
   Of course the initial section title will probably be wrong, but at the time the
   commit is created one last edit is made and the section title is updated along
   with every reference to it!
2) When starting another change for the same repo grab the commits link, such as:
   https://github.com/winksaville/vc-x1/commit/e284505c346ca051e03d36889389e49229252904
   and update the chores-xx.md section to have that link in a list of one or more references
   to the commits, something like ; `Commits: [23],[25]`

Thoughts

> AGENT

Strong yes on the direction. The principle I'd state it as: **git is the source of truth for "what changed mechanically"; `chores-NN.md` is the source of truth for "why, and the design thinking"; the two are cross-linked, neither restates the other.** Today's setup has the *commit body* mirror the chores `### Edits` list — same content, two places, and the duplication CLAUDE.md acknowledges but resolves the wrong way (it names the markdown the truth; the immutable, tooled, naturally-commit-scoped record is the better home). Your three points fix exactly that. Per-point:

**1) Drop `### Edits` from `chores-NN.md` — yes.** What stays in a chores section: the intro paragraph (now pitched a notch higher — "this step ports X to Context, no behavior change" rather than a file list), the `###` design subsections (the durable rationale a commit message is the wrong place for), and the new `Commits:` pointer. The one cost: to see "which files," a markdown reader takes one hop (click the commit link) instead of reading inline — acceptable, since CLAUDE.md already requires the commit *body* to carry the curated "file + one-line gist" list, so nothing is lost, it just lives in the one place that describes that commit. (`.claude`-side commits are unaffected — they never had the dup.)

**2) Chores heading === commit title, synced at finalize — yes, with eyes open.** Real costs:
- The heading inherits the commit-title form: `<type>: <desc> (<version>)`, ≤50 chars. So `## refactor: port push to Context (0.48.0-6)` replaces today's freer `## push → Context + PushParams (0.48.0-6)`. The descriptive richness (arrow notation, type names) moves into the intro paragraph; the heading's job becomes *identify*, not *describe*.
- "Update every reference to it" = every `[N]: /notes/chores-NN.md#anchor` back-ref (in `todo.md`, `ARCHITECTURE.md`, sibling chores files, the file itself) gets re-pointed when the heading changes. That's the "one last edit." It needs to be a pre-commit-checklist item, and a future `vc-x1 validate-repo` should check anchor integrity (dangling `#anchor` refs are the failure mode).
- Timing slots into the existing flow cleanly: do the work → draft the chores section with a provisional heading → at the message-approval gate, settle the final title → edit the chores heading + back-refs to match → `vc-x1 push <bm> --title "<that title>" --body "<the edit list>"`. The chores edit rides in the same commit it describes.

**3) `Commits: [N],[M]` ref list with the commit URL — yes.** Caveats:
- **One-commit lag.** The URL doesn't exist until `push-app` runs, so it gets backfilled in the *next* cycle's commit (your "when starting another change, grab the link" step). Accept it — the commit itself is the truth; the `Commits:` line is a browse-convenience, and a markdown reader who hits a not-yet-backfilled section just runs `git log --grep "(0.49.0-3)"`. Make "backfill the previous section's `Commits:` ref" a cycle-start checklist item. (Future: `vc-x1 push` could do the backfill itself right after `push-app`, since the SHA is known then — but that's a forward-looking enhancement, not a blocker.)
- **Host coupling.** `https://github.com/winksaville/vc-x1/commit/<sha>` rots if the repo moves. The project's already drifting toward GitHub URLs for refs (there's a todo for `blob/main/...` source refs), so it's consistent — but consider pairing it with the short SHA in the ref text so `git show <sha>` works host-independently: e.g. `[23]: https://github.com/winksaville/vc-x1/commit/e284505c… (e284505c)`. Your call.
- Placement: a `Commits: [23]` line as the first line under the heading (metadata-style, immediately visible); the `[23]: <url>` target in the file's bottom `# References` section, using the existing machinery (`[23],[25]`, not `[23][25]`).

**Open sub-decisions before this lands:**

- **Squash interaction.** When a multi-step cycle lands *squashed to one commit*, do the per-substep subsections each point `Commits:` at that one squashed SHA (5 subsections → same SHA), or collapse the subsections too? Needs a rule.

**Scoping/sequencing.** This is a CLAUDE.md + `notes/README.md` convention change — it shouldn't ride the `0.49.0` code cycle. Options:

- **(c)** Fold into `0.49.0-0` so the Migration B cycle's commits use the new convention from step one.

> AGENT

Yeah — I conflated two separate things in that earlier line. Let me untangle them.

**"Host coupling" is purely about the URL *prefix*, not the SHA.** The SHA (`e284505c346ca051e03d36889389e49229252904`) is content-addressed git data — it's identical in every clone regardless of where `origin` points. What rots is the wrapper:
- GitHub: `https://github.com/<owner>/<repo>/commit/<sha>`
- GitLab: `https://gitlab.com/<owner>/<repo>/-/commit/<sha>` ← your observation; GitLab inserts `/-/` to disambiguate project paths from sub-resources (it supports nested groups)
- self-hosted Gitea/cgit/etc.: different again

So if the project ever moves hosts, every `https://github.com/...` ref in the notes is dead. The SHA inside it isn't — `git show <sha>` still works. *That's* all I meant: don't bake only the host-specific URL into the durable record; keep the host-agnostic SHA as the real identifier. The "short SHA" remark was a tangent and a muddled one — sorry.

**Your two questions, answered:**
1. *GitHub/GitLab accepting short SHAs in URLs* — yes, both do. `https://github.com/winksaville/vc-x1/commit/e284505c34` works and GitHub 302-redirects to the canonical full URL; GitLab same. 7 chars (git's old default) works too. So a short SHA in a URL is fine *today*.
2. *"first 12 chars like ochids"* — that'd be a reasonable short convention, but note the namespaces differ: ochids are **jj change IDs** (`qqkonsnsvkqo` — base-32-ish, 12 chars by `--id-len` default), while a `Commits:` ref points at the **git commit SHA** (hex, what GitHub indexes). So it'd be an *analogy* to the ochid convention, not a reuse of it. 12 hex chars is plenty collision-safe for this repo's lifetime (48 bits; you'd need ~tens of millions of commits before it's a real risk).

**The catch with any short SHA:** a hard-coded abbreviation that's unambiguous *today* can become ambiguous as the repo grows — `git show e284505c` will eventually refuse if another object shares that prefix. The full 40-char SHA never has that problem. So short = prettier but has a (distant, theoretical) expiry; full = bulletproof and ugly.

**What I'd recommend for the `0.48.2` convention** — and this is the bit you flagged for more discussion, so push back freely:

Store the **bare full git SHA** as the `[N]:` ref target:
```
[23]: e284505c346ca051e03d36889389e49229252904
```
Rationale: it's the git-native pointer, host-agnostic, never ambiguous, `git show <sha>` follows it in any clone — and it's *consistent with the whole premise* of this change ("git is the source of truth; the markdown points, doesn't restate"). A URL is a presentation-layer convenience; baking it into the notes pulls a host dependency into the durable record.

Trade-off I won't hide: a bare SHA in a rendered `.md` on GitHub is **not** auto-linkified (GitHub auto-links SHAs in commit messages / issues / PR text, but not in repo markdown files, as far as I know — worth a 30-second test before we commit to it). So you lose one-click browsing; you'd paste the SHA into the URL bar or `git show` it.

If that clickability matters enough, the defensible alternatives:
- **SHA + URL, SHA first as the canonical part:** `[23]: e284505c346ca051e03d36889389e49229252904 — https://github.com/winksaville/vc-x1/commit/e284505c34` — belt-and-suspenders; if the host changes you fix the parenthetical, the SHA stays. Verbose but unambiguous about which half is truth.
- **URL only, full SHA in it:** `https://github.com/.../commit/e284505c34...e49229252904` — clickable now, host-coupled, SHA still hand-extractable from a dead link. Closest to the project's existing direction (there's a todo to adopt `blob/main/...` GitHub URLs for source refs).

My vote: **bare full SHA**. Second choice: **SHA + URL (SHA first)**. The pure-URL form I'd avoid for the durable record.

Want me to (a) lock one of these, or (b) do a quick test of GitHub markdown SHA auto-linking first, then decide — before I draft the `0.48.2` CLAUDE.md edits?

> AGENT

Two things — first a syntax wrinkle that bends the "full SHA first" idea slightly, then the `0.48.2` plan.

## The `Commits:` ref format

For `[N]` to *render as a clickable link* (which it does in this project — `[78]` etc. are reference-style links), the `[N]: …` definition has to put the **URL as the destination** — markdown reference-link defs are `[label]: <destination> "<optional title>"`, and `[N]` links to the destination. So we can't literally put the SHA first in the definition without breaking the click.

But the **title slot** gets us everything you asked for:

```
Commits: [1]
…
[1]: https://github.com/winksaville/vc-x1/commit/3f176b45235c "3f176b45235c4bc7da4b8de5b18a7f454c464d1e"
```

(`3f176b45235c4bc7da4b8de5b18a7f454c464d1e` is the actual `0.48.1` commit; `3f176b45235c` is its first 12 hex.)

- `[1]` → clickable → GitHub/GitLab resolve the 12-char prefix → commit page. ✓ surf-and-click
- The full 40-char SHA sits in the `"…"` — visible in the raw `.md`, so an external DB scraping the notes gets the canonical, host-agnostic identifier (and `git show 3f176b…` works in any clone). ✓ external docs / database
- URL stays short. ✓
- Host moves to GitLab/self-hosted? The full SHA in the title survives; you rewrite the destination URL (and yes, GitLab needs the `/-/` you spotted). The *truth* never depended on the host. ✓
- Uses the existing `[N]` machinery; `Commits: [1],[3]` for multi-commit. ✓

The only concession: the URL is *syntactically* first in the definition (markdown requires it); the full SHA rides in the title. But it's still right there in the source, which is what the DB/external-docs case actually needs. (The alternative — an inline `[<full-sha>](<url>)` so the SHA is the visible link text — works but bloats the section body and drops out of `# References`; I'd not.)

OK with this form? If so:

## `0.48.2` plan (single-step)

- **`Cargo.toml`**: `0.48.1` → `0.48.2`.
- **`CLAUDE.md`**:
  - `### Chores section headers` — the chores section heading is now **exactly the commit title** (`<type>: <desc> (<version>)`); set provisionally during the work, synced to the final title (and every anchor back-ref to it updated) as the last edit before `vc-x1 push`. Descriptive richness moves to the intro paragraph.
  - `### Chores: scannable bullets…` — **drop the `### Edits` subsection**. The commit message body is the source of truth for the file-by-file list. A chores section keeps: the `Commits:` line (first line under the heading), the intro paragraph, and the `###` design subsections.
  - New text — the `Commits: [N],[M]` convention: points at the git commit(s) the section describes; ref def is `[N]: <url-with-12-char-SHA> "<full-40-char-SHA>"`; backfilled when the *next* change to that repo starts (the latest section is briefly `Commits:`-less — fine, the commit itself is the record).
  - The squash interaction: a squash-to-one-commit close-out now also owns the heading/`Commits:`/anchor fixups across every squashed-away section — real work, so the bias tips further toward "keep separate."
  - `### App-repo body` — body bullets *are* the canonical change list now (not "mirrors chores"); each = file + one-line gist.
  - `### Pre-commit checklist` — reword item 8; add "sync chores heading → final commit title + fix anchor back-refs"; add a cycle-start item "backfill the previous chores section's `Commits:` ref."
- **`notes/README.md`**: note that chores `# References` now also hold commit refs in the `[N]: <url> "<full-sha>"` form.
- **`notes/chores-09.md`** (the worked example): add a `# References` section (file-local, starts `[1]`); reshape the `## por-dual parity capture + icr cleanup (0.48.1)` section → rename to `## docs: por-dual capture + icr cleanup (0.48.1)` (its real commit title), drop its `### Edits`, add `Commits: [1]` → the SHA above; **then** add the `0.48.2` section *born in the new shape* (intro + a `###` subsection capturing this convention, no `### Edits`, `Commits:` line backfilled later in `0.49.0-0`). So the file ends with one fully-converted section + one freshly-minted one — both states demonstrated.
- **`notes/todo.md`**: re-point `[92]` to the renamed 0.48.1 anchor; add the `0.48.2` `## Done` entry + its `[93]` ref (re-pointed at push time when the 0.48.2 heading/anchor settles — the convention in action).

  "new_string": "### Chores section content — no edit list; git is the record\n\nA chores section is: a `Commits:` line (first line under the\nheader — see below), a short intro paragraph (what changed and\n*why*, a notch above file-list granularity), and any `###`\ndesign subsections. The chores section\nis the source of truth for the design thinking; the two\ncross-link, neither restates the other.\n\nWhen the intro starts wanting to explain a mechanism,\nhypothesis, or wrinkle, don't inflate it — promote that to its\nown `###` subsection inside the same `chores-NN.md`. If the\nwrinkle is a live design concern (something that *should*\nchange, not just be recorded), also add a `notes/todo.md` item\nwith a `[N]` ref pointing at that subsection (todo→chores is the\nnormal ref direction).\n\n**Why:** a chores edit list and the commit body were specified\nto be the same content in two places — and detail written twice\ndrifts. Git owns the mechanical record; chores owns the\nnarrative; `Commits:` links them.\n\n### Chores commit references\n\nThe first line under a chores section header is a `Commits:`\nline citing the git commit(s) that section records:\n\n```\n## refactor: port push to Context (0.48.0-6)\n\nCommits: [3]\n\n<intro paragraph...>\n```\n\n`Commits:` uses the file-local `[N]` reference machinery (see\n[notes/README.md](notes/README.md#reference-numbering));\nmultiple commits are `Commits: [3],[5]`. The `# References`\ndefinition puts the **commit URL** as the destination (so `[N]`\nrenders as a clickable link), with the **full 40-hex SHA** in\nthe title slot:\n\n```\n[3]: https://github.com/winksaville/vc-x1/commit/<12-hex> \"<40-hex>\"\n```\n\n- The 12-hex short SHA in the URL keeps it short; GitHub /\n  GitLab resolve a unique prefix to the canonical commit page\n  (GitLab's path has a `/-/` before `commit/`).\n- The full SHA in the title is host-agnostic and unambiguous —\n  it survives a repo host change, `git show <40-hex>` works in\n  any clone, and external tooling scraping the notes (a\n  database, say) gets the canonical identifier.\n\n**Timing.** The commit doesn't exist when its chores section is\nwritten, so the `Commits:` line is **backfilled when the next\nchange to that repo is started** — the cycle-start step grabs\nthe just-pushed commit's URL + SHA and fills it in. The single\nnewest section is briefly `Commits:`-less; that's fine — the\ncommit itself is the record, and `git log --grep \"(X.Y.Z)\"`\nfinds it."
}
```

Single entry on `main`;\n  sub-step granularity preserved only in the commit body's\n  edit list. Right when the work is one logical change with\n  intermediate validation points.\n- **Keep separate (N + 1 commits)** when the decomposition is\n  itself informative (different conceptual stages, design\n  progression worth showing in `git log`). Used on\n  `0.41.1-6.7` (8 sub-sub-step commits + 1 close-out commit).\n\nPick at close-out, not at cycle start. Default is squash;\ndeviate when the artifact is the decomposition.",
  "new_string": "Two valid shapes for landing the sub-step stack on `main`:\n\n- **Squash to one cycle commit** — single entry on `main`;\n  sub-step granularity preserved only in the commit body's\n  edit list. Right when the work is one logical change with\n  intermediate validation points. **Cost:** the per-sub-step\n  chores sections collapse into one, whose header becomes the\n  single close-out commit title, and every anchor back-ref to a\n  now-gone per-sub-step section gets re-pointed — real work,\n  done as part of the squash.\n- **Keep separate (N + 1 commits)** when the decomposition is\n  itself informative (different conceptual stages, design\n  progression worth showing in `git log`). Used on\n  `0.41.1-6.7` (8 sub-sub-step commits + 1 close-out commit).\n  Each section keeps its own header / `Commits:` ref — no\n  consolidation churn.\n\nPick at close-out, not at cycle start. No firm default — weigh\nthe squash's chores-consolidation cost against a cleaner\n`git log`; that cost biases toward keeping separate unless the\ndecomposition genuinely isn't informative."

**`CLAUDE.md` changes:**
1. **`### Chores section headers`** — rewritten: a section header is now *exactly the commit title* (`<type>: <desc> (<version>)`); provisional during the work, synced to the final title — and every anchor back-ref to it updated — as the last edit before `vc-x1 push`. Notes that existing chores-01..09 sections keep their old `## Description (X.Y.Z)` headers (grandfathered); convention applies going forward; `0.48.2` converts the `0.48.1` section as the worked example.
2. **`### Chores: scannable bullets…` → `### Chores section content — no edit list; git is the record`** — a chores section = a `Commits:` line + intro paragraph + `###` design subsections, and **no per-file edit list**; that list lives in the commit message body, which is the source of truth for the mechanical record. Chores owns the narrative/design; they cross-link, neither restates.
3. **NEW `### Chores commit references`** — the `Commits: [N],[M]` first-line convention; `# References` definition form `[N]: https://…/commit/<12-hex> "<40-hex>"` (URL = clickable destination via short SHA; full 40-hex SHA in the title slot — host-agnostic, `git show`-able, scrape-able by external tooling); GitLab's `/-/` noted; backfilled at the *next* cycle's start; the single newest section is briefly `Commits:`-less (fine — the commit is the record).
4. **`### Pre-commit checklist`** — added a cycle-*start* step (backfill the previous section's `Commits:` ref); reworded item 8 (add a chores *section*, header = provisional commit title, intro + `###` subsections, no edit list); new item 9 (sync header → final commit title + fix anchor back-refs); old #9 → #10.
5. **`### App-repo body`** (Commit Message Style) — flipped: the commit body bullet list **is the source of truth** for the cycle's mechanical change record; chores carries narrative/design, not a copy.
6. **`### Cycle close-out — squash or keep separate?`** — dropped "default is squash"; added the squash *cost* (per-sub-step chores sections collapse into one, header becomes the close-out commit title, anchor back-refs re-pointed); "no firm default — that cost biases toward keeping separate unless the decomposition genuinely isn't informative."
7. **`### Commit-first review model`** step 6 — squashing a follow-up that changes the title now also moves the chores header + its anchor back-refs.

**`Cargo.toml` / `Cargo.lock`**: `0.48.1` → `0.48.2`.

+## docs: chores edit list → commit message (0.48.2)
+
+Move the per-file edit list out of `chores-NN.md` sections
+into the commit message body — git becomes the source of
+truth for what each commit changed mechanically. A chores
+section now keeps only the narrative + `###` design
+subsections, takes its header from the commit title, and
+cites its commit(s) via a `Commits:` ref. CLAUDE.md and
+`notes/README.md` codify it; the `0.48.1` section above is
+converted as the worked example, this section is born in the
+new shape (its own `Commits:` ref backfilled at the next
+cycle's start).
+
+### Why git owns the edit record
+
+- **DRY.** The old setup had the commit body mirror a chores
+  `### Edits` list — the same content twice, which drifts.
+  Git's copy is immutable, `git show`-able, commit-scoped; it
+  wins as canonical. Chores keeps what git is bad at — the
+  why.
+- **`Commits:` ref shape.** Markdown reference-link
+  definitions put the destination first, so to keep `[N]`
+  clickable the URL is the destination
+  (`…/commit/<12-hex>` — short, and GitHub / GitLab resolve a
+  unique prefix), and the full 40-hex SHA rides in the title
+  slot: host-agnostic, unambiguous, and present in the raw
+  markdown for external tooling (a database, say) to scrape.
+- **Backfill timing.** A commit's URL/SHA don't exist when
+  its section is written, so `Commits:` is filled in at the
+  *next* cycle's start — the newest section is briefly
+  ref-less, which is fine (the commit itself is the record).
+
+# References
+
+[1]: https://github.com/winksaville/vc-x1/commit/3f176b45235c "3f176b45235c4bc7da4b8de5b18a7f454c464d1e"

-  terse bullet list. Each bullet corresponds one-to-one with the
-  edits structure already documented in `notes/chores-*.md` for
-  this step — just the file and a one-line gist (e.g.
-  `README.md: new Overview intro`). Do *not* restate the detail
-  that lives in chores; the commit body is a scan-able index, not
-  a duplicate. The chores section is the source of truth.
+  terse bullet list — one bullet per file changed, the file plus a
+  one-line gist (e.g. `README.md: new Overview intro`). This list
+  **is the source of truth** for the cycle's mechanical change
+  record; the chores section carries the narrative + design, not a
+  copy of it. Keep bullets terse — a scannable index, not prose;
+  promote any "why" to a chores `###` subsection.
-### Chores section headers
-
-Chores section headers use trailing version format:
-
-```
-## Description (X.Y.Z)
-```
-
-Example: `` ## Add `fn claude-symlink` (0.27.0) ``
-
-### Chores: scannable bullets, detail in subsections
-
-A per-step chores subsection opens with a short intro, then a
-`-` bullet list that mirrors the commit body — one line per
-file/edit, a scannable index. When a bullet starts wanting to
-explain *why* something is the way it is, or to flag a wrinkle,
-don't inflate the bullet:
-
-- Promote the explanation to its own `###` subsection inside the
-  same `chores-NN.md`; point the bullet at it with a markdown
-  anchor link (`[gist](#the-subsection-heading)`).
-- If the wrinkle is a live design concern — something that
-  *should* change, not just be recorded — also add a
-  `notes/todo.md` item, with a `[N]` ref pointing at that
-  subsection (todo→chores is the normal ref direction).
-
-**Why:** verbose bullets bury the index, and detail written
-twice (bullet + subsection) drifts. One source of truth, linked
-from wherever it's relevant.
+### Headings and entries that record a commit
+
+A `chores-NN.md` `##` section header that records a specific
+commit, the matching `todo.md > ## Done` entry, and any `[N]`
+reference to that section all use **exactly that commit's
+title** — `<type>: <desc> (<version>)`, the same string the
+commit gets (see [Commit Message Style](#commit-message-style)).
+E.g. the chores header `## refactor: port push to Context
+(0.48.0-6)` and the Done line `- refactor: port push to Context
+(0.48.0-6) [3]`. For a multi-step cycle the `## Done` entry uses
+the close-out commit's title.
+
+This does **not** apply to organizational headings (`## Todo`,
+`## In Progress`, `# References`) or to design `###` subsections
+inside a chores section — those are named for whatever fits.
+Among the commit-recording ones, exact match is the strong
+default (nothing absolute): a near-miss just makes it harder to
+line a record up with its commit.
+
+A commit-recording header is provisional while the work is in
+progress; the *last* edit before `vc-x1 push` syncs it — and the
+`## Done` entry / `[N]` anchor for that commit — to the final
+commit title. See [Markdown anchor links](#markdown-anchor-links)
+for the slug algorithm; the pre-commit checklist catches a
+dangling `#anchor`, and a future `vc-x1 validate-repo` should too
+(and should verify the recorded title matches the commit).
+
+Existing sections and `## Done` entries (most of chores-01..09;
+the pre-`0.48.1` `## Done` lines) predate this and keep their
+free-form text; the convention applies going forward — `0.48.2`
+converts the `0.48.1` section + its Done entry as the worked
+example.
+
+### Chores section content — no edit list; git is the record
+
+A chores section is: a `Commits:` line (first line under the
+header — see below), a short intro paragraph (what changed and
+*why*, a notch above file-list granularity), and any `###`
+design subsections. It does **not** carry a per-file edit list —
+that lives in the commit message body, which is the source of
+truth for "what changed mechanically" (immutable, `git
+show`-able, naturally scoped to the commit). The chores section
+is the source of truth for the design thinking; the two
+cross-link, neither restates the other.
+
+When the intro starts wanting to explain a mechanism,
+hypothesis, or wrinkle, don't inflate it — promote that to its
+own `###` subsection inside the same `chores-NN.md`. If the
+wrinkle is a live design concern (something that *should*
+change, not just be recorded), also add a `notes/todo.md` item
+with a `[N]` ref pointing at that subsection (todo→chores is the
+normal ref direction).
+
+**Why:** a chores edit list and the commit body were specified
+to be the same content in two places — and detail written twice
+drifts. Git owns the mechanical record; chores owns the
+narrative; `Commits:` links them.
+
+### Chores commit references
+
+The first line under a chores section header is a `Commits:`
+line citing the git commit(s) that section records:
+
+```
+## refactor: port push to Context (0.48.0-6)
+
+Commits: [3]
+
+<intro paragraph...>
+```
+
+`Commits:` uses the file-local `[N]` reference machinery (see
+[notes/README.md](notes/README.md#reference-numbering));
+multiple commits are `Commits: [3],[5]`. The `# References`
+definition puts the **commit URL** as the destination (so `[N]`
+renders as a clickable link), with the **full 40-hex SHA** in
+the title slot:
+
+```
+[3]: https://github.com/winksaville/vc-x1/commit/<12-hex> "<40-hex>"
+```
+
+- The 12-hex short SHA in the URL keeps it short; GitHub /
+  GitLab resolve a unique prefix to the canonical commit page
+  (GitLab's path has a `/-/` before `commit/`).
+- The full SHA in the title is host-agnostic and unambiguous —
+  it survives a repo host change, `git show <40-hex>` works in
+  any clone, and external tooling scraping the notes (a
+  database, say) gets the canonical identifier.
+
+**Timing.** The commit doesn't exist when its chores section is
+written, so the `Commits:` line is **backfilled when the next
+change to that repo is started** — the cycle-start step grabs
+the just-pushed commit's URL + SHA and fills it in. The single
+newest section is briefly `Commits:`-less; that's fine — the
+commit itself is the record, and `git log --grep "(X.Y.Z)"`
+finds it.
+At cycle *start* (before the version bump): backfill the
+previous chores section's `Commits:` ref with the just-pushed
+commit's URL + full SHA — see
+[Chores commit references](#chores-commit-references).
+
-8. Update `notes/chores-*.md` — add a subsection describing the change
-9. Update `notes/README.md` — if functionality changed (new flags,
-   new subcommands, changed behavior)

+9. Sync the chores section header to the **final** commit title
+   and update every markdown anchor back-reference to it.

+## docs: chores edit list → commit message (0.48.2)
+
+Move the per-file edit list out of `chores-NN.md` sections
+into the commit message body — git becomes the source of
+truth for what each commit changed mechanically. A chores
+section now keeps only the narrative + `###` design
+subsections, cites its commit(s) via a `Commits:` ref, and —
+when it records a commit, as does a `todo.md > ## Done` entry —
+takes its header verbatim from that commit's title
+(organizational headings and design subsections don't).
+CLAUDE.md and `notes/README.md` codify it; the `0.48.1`
+section here and its `todo.md > ## Done` entry are converted
+as the worked example, this section is born in the new shape
+(its own `Commits:` ref backfilled at the next cycle's start).
+
+### Why git owns the edit record
+
+- **DRY.** The old setup had the commit body mirror a chores
+  `### Edits` list — the same content twice, which drifts.
+  Git's copy is immutable, `git show`-able, commit-scoped; it
+  wins as canonical. Chores keeps what git is bad at — the
+  why.
+- **`Commits:` ref shape.** Markdown reference-link
+  definitions put the destination first, so to keep `[N]`
+  clickable the URL is the destination
+  (`…/commit/<12-hex>` — short, and GitHub / GitLab resolve a
+  unique prefix), and the full 40-hex SHA rides in the title
+  slot: host-agnostic, unambiguous, and present in the raw
+  markdown for external tooling (a database, say) to scrape.
+- **Backfill timing.** A commit's URL/SHA don't exist when
+  its section is written, so `Commits:` is filled in at the
+  *next* cycle's start — the newest section is briefly
+  ref-less, which is fine (the commit itself is the record).
+

+

> AGENT

On the GitHub test — I'd skip it. I'm ~90% sure GitHub renders `[[27]]` as `[`+link+`]` with the link working (it follows CommonMark, where the inner `[27]` is a valid shortcut reference link). But you've *already* learned the thing that matters: `[[N]]` isn't universally safe — Zed breaks the inner link. A throwaway commit to confirm GitHub wouldn't change the conclusion, and it'd publish a scratch commit to GitHub's cache/index just for a rendering check. Not worth it.

So the real decision is: `[[N]]` (visible brackets, but link-fragile across viewers) vs plain `[N]` (no visible brackets, but links work in *every* CommonMark renderer). My recommendation: **go back to plain `[N]`.**

- Robustness wins for durable docs. A notes convention that silently breaks links in one of the two editors you use — and that we'd be *guessing* about for GitHub and any future viewer — is a bad trade for a marginal clarity gain.
- "Brackets disappear when rendered" is the standard, already-lived-with behavior of markdown reference links — the existing notes are full of `[80],[88]`-style trailing refs that render as bare clickable numbers. `Commits: 1` reads fine in context (the `Commits:` label + the bottom `# References` section make it obvious it's a ref); it's not worth fragility to dress it up.
- The *only* robust way to get visible brackets is the full-reference form `[\[1\]][1]` (link text `\[1\]` → renders `[1]`, reference label `1` → resolves against `[1]:`). It works everywhere — but it's noticeably uglier in the source (`Commits: [\[1\]][1], [\[3\]][3]`), and I'd not. Not worth it.

If you agree, I'll: revert the `[[…]]` edits (your `chores-09.md` `Commits: [[1]]`, your `todo.md` `[[27]]` / `[[28]],[[29]]`, my CLAUDE.md `[[3]]` example) back to single brackets, and add a one-liner to CLAUDE.md's `### Chores commit references` noting that the citation renders without surrounding brackets (expected; the `Commits:` label disambiguates) — so future-me doesn't re-litigate this.

(Counter-position, if you'd rather: keep `[[N]]` and accept Zed's broken link as a local-editor quirk, betting that GitHub — the canonical public view — handles it. Defensible if you weight GitHub-rendering over Zed-editing. But I'd take the robust option.)

Your call — revert to `[N]`, or keep `[[N]]`?

Ha — so the Zed "broken link" was a phantom: Zed's markdown *preview* just doesn't navigate **any** links (single `[N]` or `[[N]]` alike — it underlines/colors them but they're not clickable). So `[[N]]` isn't worse than `[N]` anywhere; it's strictly "visible brackets where the renderer supports clickable links (GitHub, VS Code), neutral where it doesn't (Zed preview)." The only real residual caveat is an Obsidian-style renderer that treats `[[…]]` as wiki-link syntax — and none of the project's viewers do that.

Given the downside evaporated and you like the brackets: I say **keep `[[N]]`**. It's a small, harmless, you-prefer-it improvement.

Scope, if you agree:
- **`Commits:` lines** — `[[N]]` (already codified in CLAUDE.md's `### Chores commit references`; your `chores-09.md` `Commits: [[1]]` matches).
- **Going-forward citations** generally — new `## Done` entries, new todo items, chores cross-refs use `[[N]]`. So I'd flip the two `0.48.2` Done entries → `[[92]]` / `[[93]]`, the example in `### Headings and entries that record a commit` → `[[3]]`, and update `notes/README.md`'s citation examples (`[[1]]`, `[[2]],[[3]]`) + CLAUDE.md's `### Notes references` line.
- **`todo.md`** — it's small and actively maintained, so I'd full-retrofit it to `[[N]]` now (your `[[27]]` / `[[28]],[[29]]` test edits + the rest), so it's uniform rather than mixed. The `# References` *definitions* stay `[N]:` (only the citations get the doubled brackets — I'll be careful not to touch the `[N]:` lines).
- **`chores-01..09`** — grandfathered (big, stable; not worth the churn). Existing `[N]` citations there stay as-is.

Confirm that scope and I'll do it as part of `0.48.2`. (Or, if "more trouble than it's worth" wins after all, say so and I'll revert the `[[…]]` edits — yours in `chores-09.md`/`todo.md` and my `[[3]]` in CLAUDE.md — back to `[N]`, and add a CLAUDE.md one-liner that bracketless rendering is expected.)

> DEVELOPER

agree, update todo.md in total and grandfather chores, the convention should be "universal", i.e. we change markdown ref/anchors as we'll as inline URLs?

> DEVELOPER

agree, update todo.md in total and grandfather chores, the convention should be "universal", i.e. we change markdown anchors as we'll as inline URLs?

> AGENT

On "universal" — I'd say **no, scope it to reference-style citations** (`[[N]]`, `[[N]],[[M]]`), and leave inline links (`[text](url)`, `[text](#anchor)`) alone. Reasoning:

- The "brackets disappear when rendered" problem only hits **reference citations** whose text is a bare number — `[27]` renders as `27`, which doesn't *read* as "a reference to something" (could be a count, a version fragment, anything). Doubling fixes exactly that.
- An inline link's text is a descriptive phrase — `[the Overview intro](url)` renders as `the Overview intro`, link-styled. It already reads as a link; wrapping it → `[the Overview intro]` just clutters prose for ~no gain.
- And in *raw source* (no preview), inline/anchor links already show their brackets (`[the thing](url)`); only reference *citations* lose bracket-ness in the rendered view (source `[N]` → rendered `N`). So the doubling targets the one form that actually drops brackets.

So the convention I'd codify: **reference-style numeric citations are double-bracketed (`[[N]]`) so they render as `[27]`, not `27`; `[N]:` definitions and inline `[text](url)` / `[text](#anchor)` links are unchanged.**

If you agree with that scope, I'll do:
- **`todo.md`** — full-retrofit every `[N]` / `[N],[M]` citation → `[[N]]` (the `[N]:` definition lines and any inline links untouched).
- **`0.48.2` Done entries** → `[[92]]` / `[[93]]`; the example in `### Headings and entries that record a commit` → `[[3]]`.
- **`notes/README.md`** "Todo format" examples → `[[1]]`, `[[2]],[[3]]`; **CLAUDE.md** `### Notes references` line to match; the scoping note added (citations doubled, definitions/inline links not). (`### Chores commit references` already shows `Commits: [[3]]`.)
- **`chores-01..09`** — grandfathered, untouched.

Confirm the scope (citations only, not inline links) and I'll run the retrofit as part of `0.48.2`. Or if you really want it everywhere, I'll do that — but I'd advise against, per the above.

> DEVELOPER

Agree, (I thought universal might be easier, inline is "bigger" so they are easy to see)

45	Todo.md contains two main sections "Todo" and "Done" each item is a
46	short explanations of a tasks and links to more details using 1 or more
47	references.
48	
49	Todo items use lazy numbering — every entry begins with `1. ` and the
50	markdown renderer auto-numbers them. Reorder or insert items without
51	renumbering, and reference an entry by its displayed number ("let's
52	work on #3"). The Done section keeps `-` bullets — items aren't
53	referenced by number once completed.
54	
55	Multiple references must be separated: `[2],[3]` not `[2,3]` or `[2][3]`.
56	In markdown, `[2,3]` is a single ref key (won't resolve) and `[2][3]`
57	is parsed as display text `2` with ref key `3` (so `[2]` won't resolve).
58	
59	Examples:
60	
61	# Todo
62	- Add new feature X [details](features.md#feature-x)
63	- Fix bug Y [1]
64	
65	# Done
66	- Fixed issue Z [2],[3]
67	
68	[1]: bugs.md#bug-y
69	[2]: issues.md#issue-z
70	[3]: fixes.md#fix-z
71

```
### Why git owns the edit record

- **DRY.** The old setup had the commit body mirror a chores
  `### Edits` list — the same content twice, which drifts.
  Git's copy is immutable, `git show`-able, commit-scoped; it
  wins as canonical. Chores keeps what git is bad at — the
  why.
- **`Commits:` ref shape.** Markdown reference-link
  definitions put the destination first, so to keep `[N]`
  clickable the URL is the destination
  (`…/commit/<12-hex>` — short, and GitHub / GitLab resolve a
  unique prefix), and the full 40-hex SHA rides in the title
  slot: host-agnostic, unambiguous, and present in the raw
  markdown for external tooling (a database, say) to scrape.
- **Backfill timing.** A commit's URL/SHA don't exist when
  its section is written, so `Commits:` is filled in at the
  *next* cycle's start — the newest section is briefly
  ref-less, which is fine (the commit itself is the record).

# References
```

Hmm — let me push back gently, because I think the file's actually mostly OK and the fix is small. I grepped every `[N]` in `chores-09.md`:

- **Lines 38–40, 88, 149, 180, 314–315, 683, 743, 817, 973–974** — every one of those `[N]` mentions is inside a **code span** (`` `[72]` ``, `` `[76]→[78]` ``, `` `[86]` ``, …). They're in the grandfathered `### Edits` lists, and they're not citations — they're literal text *quoting* a ref-key from **another file** ("added a `[86]` ref *to todo.md*", "`[72]→[76]` renumbering *in todo.md/chores-08*"). Code spans never become links, so these render as monospace `[72]` text regardless — which is exactly what's intended. Adding `[72]:`…`[88]:` definitions to `chores-09`'s `# References` wouldn't do anything for them (code spans don't resolve refs), and conceptually they're *not* `chores-09`-local refs anyway.
- **Line 114** — the one genuine exception: `Design references stay at chores-07 [76]:` has a *bare* `[76]` (not in a code span). With no `[76]:` def in `chores-09` it renders as literal `[76]` text — so it's not "broken," just inconsistent with the rest of the file (every other `[N]` here is code-spanned).
- **Line 978 / 1029** — `Commits: [[1]]` ↔ `[1]: …` — the real citation, resolves. ✓
- **Line 1022** — `` `[[N]]` (not `[N]`) … `[27]` `` — code-span examples *describing the convention*, not citations. ✓

So `chores-09`'s `# References` having just `[1]:` is correct — that's the only thing `chores-09` actually cites (the `Commits:` ref). It's not missing 70+ defs; it's missing zero (well, modulo line 114).

**Proposed fix** (`0.48.3`, small):
- Line 114: wrap `[76]` in a code span (`` `[76]` ``) so it matches the file's pervasive style — it's "quoting the `[76]` ref that `todo.md` tracks", same as every other `[N]` in the file.
- Add a one-liner to `notes/README.md`'s `## Reference numbering` (and/or a note in `chores-09`'s `# References`): a `[N]` written *inside a code span* is a quoted ref-key, not a citation — needs no definition; it may belong to another file's namespace.

That's it — a code-span wrap + a clarifying note. Want me to do that as `0.48.3`?

(If you genuinely want every `[72]`…`[88]` mention in `chores-09`'s `### Edits` lists turned into working links, that's a bigger and, I'd argue, wrong move — it means un-code-spanning historical edit descriptions and pointing them at other files' chores sections, while the `### Edits` convention is being sunset anyway. I'd not. But say the word if that's what you meant.)

> AGENT

Not pushing back — you're right, and the framing is sharper than mine. Ref numbers as **footnotes** is exactly the right model:

- A `[N]` is a *file-local slot* — its only meaning is "the Nth entry in *this* file's `# References`." Two files' `[N]`s are unrelated slots that happen to share a digit. (notes/README.md already says "reference numbers are scoped to that file" — your framing makes the consequence explicit.)
- Therefore: writing `[77]` in `chores-09` to mean "todo.md's ref 77" is a category error — `chores-09`'s namespace doesn't have a `[77]` and shouldn't. If `chores-09` ever wants to *cite* the target todo.md's `[77]` points at, it picks its own next-available number (e.g. `[2]`) and defines it locally — coincidence with todo's `[77]` is just coincidence.

So those `### Edits` mentions of `[77]` / `[81]` / `[84]` / etc. in `chores-09` aren't just vestigial — they're using **foreign-namespace numbers as if they were local**, which is the same category of mistake as your earlier "looks like a ref but doesn't act like one." Deleting them is the right move under the principle, not just a polish.

The one nuance I'd preserve — and this is a *refinement* of your principle, not a counter:

- A **citation** (bare `[N]` / `[[N]]` parsed as a markdown ref-link) is file-local — never use another file's number.
- A **literal identifier** (`` `[N]` `` in a code span — monospace text, doesn't parse as a ref) is just data, like quoting a variable name. The renumbering description on line 38-40 (`` `[72]→[76]` `` in `### What needed real work`) is *naming* historical tokens from todo.md's namespace at the time of the rebase. It's not a citation; it's narrating a renumbering event, where the numbers *are* the content. Code-spans correctly say "literal token, not a ref into this file." Keep that one.

That tightens the `0.48.3` cleanup to:
- **Delete** vestigial `[N]` mentions in `chores-09`'s `### Edits` lists (~7 sites): they were using foreign-namespace numbers as if local. Reword clauses; the commit bodies carry the specifics for anyone who needs them.
- **Code-span** line 114's bare `[76]` (literal identifier, matches the survivors).
- **Keep** lines 38–40 (the renumbering description; substantive narration of historical tokens, correctly code-spanned).
- **Codify** in `notes/README.md`'s `## Reference numbering`: the footnote framing — file-local slots, citations never reuse another file's number; a `` `[N]` `` in a code span is a literal identifier (data), not a citation; if you want to cite a target a sibling file references, pick your own next-local number and define it.

Scope still `chores-09` only. (`chores-01..08` have similar vestigial mentions but no `# References` to make them glaring; their `### Edits` lists are being sunset by the 0.48.2 convention anyway. Touch them later or never.)

Sound? If so I'll start `0.48.3`.

So the bare `[76]` on line 114 is a **foreign-namespace citation attempt** — the writer was effectively saying "see ref `[76]` [in todo.md]," which is exactly the category error we just defined.

Code-spanning it would have papered over the broken citation with a "literal identifier" label that doesn't really fit — the writer's intent there was *to point at the section*, not *to name an identifier as data*. So that's apply-the-principle-inconsistently. Two honest options:

- **(a) Delete the `[76]` and rephrase.** "Design references stay at chores-07:" — loses the precise section pointer; reader has to know which section in chores-07 the deferred items map to (probably fine in context, since `chores-07 [76]:` is followed immediately by the bulleted list of those very items).
- **(b) Replace with a proper inline link to the section.** "Design references stay at [chores-07's `--scope` enum refactor section](../chores-07.md#--scope-enum-refactor-0420):" — preserves the specificity, fully working link, no foreign-namespace digit. **My pick** — inline links are exactly the "works or doesn't exist" form your principle wants.

Lines 38–40 stay as-is — those ARE literal-identifier-as-data: the renumbering event is what's being narrated, the numbers are the substance, code-spans correctly say "naming historical tokens." Different category from line 114.

So revised `0.48.3`:
- **Line 114**: option (b) — inline-link to the chores-07 section.
- **`### Edits` vestigials (~7 sites)**: delete the `[N]` parts.
- **Lines 38–40**: leave.
- **`notes/README.md` `## Reference numbering`**: codify the footnote framing (file-local slots; citation = bare `[N]` / `[[N]]`, never uses another file's number; literal-identifier = `` `[N]` `` in a code span, fine as data; cross-file pointing → inline link with anchor).

Sound? Sorry for the earlier sloppiness — the principle's clean, my application of it on line 114 wasn't.

> AGENT

Y in principle — your "fix by renumbering" is the right move and honors the footnote framing. One subtlety I should surface before I do it, though, because it splits the cleanup into two categories:

**Many of the `### Edits` mentions would resolve self-referentially.** Pattern: a chores section X's `### Edits` mentions a ref-key that — in todo.md or done.md — points right back at X itself. Specifically:

- `[85]` ref added (in the 0.45.0 chores section) → `chores-09.md#architecture-doc-and-terminology-reconciliation-0450` = that same 0.45.0 section.
- `[86]` (in 0.46.0) → that 0.46.0 section.
- `[88]` (in 0.48.0) → that 0.48.0 section.
- `[79]` (in 0.42.0-4.7) → that section. `[81]` (in 0.42.0 close-out) → that section. `[84]` (in 0.43.0-0) → that section.

Renumbering these locally still works mechanically — `[N]: …#same-section-im-in` — but the resulting click is a no-op (you click and land where you already are). That's "working" only in the technical sense.

Cross-pointing mentions, on the other hand, *do* benefit from the fix:

- Line 114's `[76]` → chores-07's `--scope` enum refactor section. ✓
- `[80]` → `/ARCHITECTURE.md`. ✓
- `[82]` / `[83]` → `forks-multi-user.md` / `bot-data-formats.md`. ✓
- `[77]` / `[78]` → chores-07 sections. ✓

So I'd propose a small refinement of strict "fix all":

- **Cross-pointing mentions** → renumber locally + define in `chores-09`'s `# References`. Useful clicks.
- **Self-referential mentions** → delete (the click wouldn't add value; the section already *is* what the ref denotes).
- **Line 38–40** (renumbering narration) → leave code-spanned (literal historical tokens; `[72]`/`[73]`/`[74]` got renumbered away and have no stable current target anyway).

Specifically: line 973-974's mixed list ("received the five migrated entries + `[77]` `[78]` `[79]` `[81]` `[84]` refs") splits — `[77]`/`[78]` cross-point (chores-07), `[79]`/`[81]`/`[84]` self-reference (chores-09 sections); reword to keep the useful pointers and drop the self-refs.

Net result: ~6 cross-pointing fixes get chores-09-local `[2]..[7]` slots + defs; ~6 self-referential mentions get pruned. The principle is fully honored (every citation works, none are no-ops), and chores-09 stops carrying useless link-clicks.

y/n on this split? If y, I'll start `0.48.3`.

Concrete numbers from the recon:

- **14 `### Edits` subsections** in chores-09 (lines 83, 143, 161, 309, 342, 370, 410, 450, 498, 593, 656, 711, 812, 961).
- **22 commit-recording sections** in chores-09 (plus one stub at line 506 that has no commit — `## Subcommand-layer architecture — moved to ARCHITECTURE.md`).
- **Commit titles + SHAs all looked up cleanly** via `jj log` — every section maps to a known commit. Headings range from minor renames (just add `chore:`/`refactor:` prefix) to substantial rewrites (`## InitParams implementation (0.44.0)` → `## refactor: introduce Context + InitParams (0.44.0)`).
- **~10 back-refs to cascade**: 4 in todo.md (`[85]`/`[86]`/`[87]`/`[88]`), 3 in done.md (`[79]`/`[81]`/`[84]`), 2 prose mentions in `init-clone-refactor-conflict.md` (lines 12, 121). `[89]`/`[90]` in todo.md point at design `###` sub-subsections inside chores-09 (not commit-recording), so they're unaffected.

So **option A** (full retro convert) = 22 heading renames + 14 `### Edits` deletions + 22 `Commits: [[N]]` first-lines + 22 `[N]:` defs in chores-09's `# References` (locally renumbered `[2]..[23]`) + ~10 back-ref updates + the line 114 fix + the `notes/README.md` footnote-framing note. Substantial chunk — call it ~45–60 min of careful editing — but every piece is mechanical and bounded; commit titles + SHAs already mapped.

**Option B** (delete `### Edits` only, leave headings) = 14 deletions + line 114 fix + footnote framing. ~15–20 min. Headings stay old-shape, no `Commits:` links, back-refs untouched.

My honest take: **A is the right call** — your principle is "references should work," and that extends naturally to "every chores section is paired with its commit via `Commits:`." It's the convention applied uniformly to one file (chores-09) so it stops being a half-converted artifact. The cost is real but tractable, and the commit-title/SHA lookups are already done. After this, chores-09 = uniformly new-shape, line 38-40 the only code-spanned historical narration, `# References` cleanly indexed with 22 commit refs.

B is the perfectly defensible "good enough" fallback if you'd rather not invest the time.

headings, insert Commits: [[N]] line\nerrors = []\nfor old, new, n, sha in SECTIONS:\n    needle = old + \"\\n\\n\"\n    if content.count(needle) != 1:\n        errors.append(f\"pattern {old!r}\\\\n\\\\n count={content.count(needle)}\")\n        continue\n    content = content.replace(needle, new + f\"\\n\\nCommits: [[{n}]]\\n\\n\", 1)\n\nif errors:\n    print(\"ERRORS:\")\n    for e Consume leading blank line(s) too.\nedits_count_before = len(re.findall(r\"^### Edits\", content, flags=re.M))\ncontent = re.sub(\n    r\"\\n*### Edits[^\\n]*\\n.*?(?=\\n## |\\Z)\",\n    \"\",\n    content,\n    flags=re.DOTALL\n)\nedits_count_after = len(re.findall(r\"^### Edits\", content, flags=re.M))\nprint(f\"### Edits subsections: {edits_count_before} → {edits_count_after}\")\n\n# Step 3: backfill 0.48.2's Commits ref\nold_482 = \"## docs: chores edit list → commit message (0.48.2)\\n\\n\"\nnew_482 = \"## docs: chores edit list → commit message (0.48.2)\\n\\nCommits: [[23]]\\n\\n\"\nassert content.count(old_482) == 1, \"0.48.2 heading not unique\"\ncontent = content.replace(old_482, new_482, 1)\n\n# Step 4: line 114 fix — bare [76] → [[24]]\nold_114 = \"Design references stay\\nat chores-07 [76]:\"\nnew_114 = \"Design references stay\\nat chores-07 [[24]]:\"\nassert content.count(old_114) == 1, \"line 114 not unique\"\ncontent = content.replace(old_114, new_114, 1)\n\n# Step 5: extend # References with [2]..[24]\nold_refs = '[1]: https://github.com/winksaville/vc-x1/commit/3f176b45235c \"3f176b45235c4bc7da4b8de5b18a7f454c464d1e\"\\n'\nnew_lines = [old_refs]\nfor old, new, n, sha in SECTIONS:\n    short = sha[:12]\n    new_lines.append(f'[{n}]: https://github.com/winksaville/vc-x1/commit/{short} \"{sha}\"\\n')\nnew_lines.append(f'[23]: https://github.com/winksaville/vc-x1/commit/{OPEN_482_SHA[:12]} \"{OPEN_482_SHA}\"\\n')\nnew_lines.append(f'[24]: /notes/chores-07.md#--scope-enum-refactor-0420\\n')\nnew_refs = \"\".join(new_lines)\nassert content.count(old_refs) == 1, \"[1] ref line not unique\"\ncontent = content.replace(old_refs, new_refs, 1)\n\nwith open(PATH, 'w') as f:\n    f.write(content)\n\nprint(\"done.\")\n"

## docs: chores edit list → commit message (0.48.2)

Commits: [[23]]

Move the per-file edit list out of `chores-NN.md` sections
into the commit message body — git becomes the source of
truth for what each commit changed mechanically. A chores
section now keeps only the narrative + `###` design
subsections, cites its commit(s) via a `Commits:` ref, and —
when it records a commit, as does a `todo.md > ## Done` entry —
takes its header verbatim from that commit's title
(organizational headings and design subsections don't).
CLAUDE.md and `notes/README.md` codify it; the `0.48.1`
section here and its `todo.md > ## Done` entry are converted
as the worked example, this section is born in the new shape
(its own `Commits:` ref backfilled at the next cycle's start).

### Why git owns the edit record

- **DRY.** The old setup had the commit body mirror a chores
  `### Edits` list — the same content twice, which drifts.
  Git's copy is immutable, `git show`-able, commit-scoped; it
  wins as canonical. Chores keeps what git is bad at — the
  why.
- **`Commits:` ref shape.** Markdown reference-link
  definitions put the destination first, so to keep the
  `[[N]]` citation clickable the `[N]:` definition's
  destination is the URL (`…/commit/<12-hex>` — short, and
  GitHub / GitLab resolve a unique prefix), and the full
  40-hex SHA rides in the title slot: host-agnostic,
  unambiguous, and present in the raw markdown for external
  tooling (a database, say) to scrape.
- **Backfill timing.** A commit's URL/SHA don't exist when
  its section is written, so `Commits:` is filled in at the
  *next* cycle's start — the newest section is briefly
  ref-less, which is fine (the commit itself is the record).
- **Doubled citations.** Reference *citations* are written
  `[[N]]` (not `[N]`) so the brackets render — `[27]` reads
  as a reference, `27` doesn't. `[N]:` definitions and inline
  links stay single-bracketed. `todo.md` is fully retrofitted;
  `chores-01..09` are grandfathered.

# References
```

## Reference numbering

Every note file (`todo.md`, `chores-NN.md`, `done.md`) keeps a
file-local `# References` section at the bottom. Reference numbers
are scoped to that file — `[1]` in `chores-07.md` and `[1]` in
`chores-01.md` are independent slots that may point at completely
different URLs. New chores files start their numbering at `[1]`.

A `chores-NN.md` `# References` entry is usually a
`/notes/<file>.md#anchor` (or `/ARCHITECTURE.md`) path, but may
also be a **commit reference** —
`[N]: <commit-url-with-12-hex-SHA> "<full-40-hex-SHA>"`, cited by
a section's `Commits:` line. See
[`../CLAUDE.md`](../CLAUDE.md#chores-commit-references) for the
why and the exact shape.

## Retiring Done entries

Reference numbers\nare scoped to that file — `[1]` in `chores-07.md` and `[1]` in\n`chores-01.md` are independent slots that may point at completely\ndifferent URLs. New chores files start their numbering at `[1]`.\n\nTreat `[N]` like a **footnote**: the number is a local slot, only\nmeaningful within its file's `# References`. So a `[N]` *citation*\n(bare `[N]`, or doubled `[[N]]` — see below) never reuses another\nfile's number; to cite a target a sibling file references, pick\nyour own next-local slot and define it (the same target may carry\na different number in each file). A `[N]` *inside a code span*\n(`` `[72]` ``) is different — that's a quoted identifier: literal\ntext naming a ref-key (often from another file's namespace), data,\nnot a citation; it needs no definition here. > DEVELOPER

I'd like to suggest we renumber the todo.md references

> AGENT

Good idea — it fits the footnote framing we just settled (a footnote section should be a compact `[1]..[N]`, not a gappy `[4],[5],[27],…[94]` left over from the rolling-buffer history).

Two questions before I do it, plus a sequencing call:

**Sequencing.** I'd do it as **`0.48.4`**, not fold it into `0.48.3`. `0.48.3` is "chores-09 → new shape" — a coherent unit, done and ready for your review now — and a todo.md renumber doesn't fit that title. The renumber churns ~34 `[N]:` definitions plus ~40 `[[N]]` citations across `## Todo`/`## Done`; it deserves its own focused commit (`docs: renumber todo.md references (0.48.4)`). Folding it in would just delay `0.48.3` and muddy its scope. (If you'd rather fold it in, I can — say the word and I'll rename `0.48.3` accordingly.)

**Order.** When renumbering, two options:
- **First-citation-appearance order** — `[1]` = the ref cited by the first `## Todo` item, `[2]` = next-new-ref-cited, …, then `## Done` items. The `# References` section ends up in that order. This is the "proper" footnote convention.
- **Compact the gaps, keep current order** — `[4]→[1]`, `[5]→[2]`, `[27]→[3]`, … (the `# References` section is already sorted numerically, so this is mechanical: just close the gaps, relative order unchanged).

I'd lean **first-citation-appearance order** (it's what makes the section read like real footnotes), but compact-the-gaps is simpler and fine if you prefer.

**Scope.** Just `todo.md`? Or also `done.md` (it's gappy too — `[4],[5],[27],…[88]` in its own file-local namespace)? `chores-09`'s `# References` is already a compact `[1]..[24]` after `0.48.3`, so it's done.

(And `0.48.3` is ready for review-before-commit regardless — let me know when you've looked and I'll prep its title/body.)

> DEVELOPER

It should only effect [[xx]] and [xx]: in todo.md no changes to any other file, correct?

> AGENT

Correct. Renumbering `todo.md`'s references is purely file-local — it touches only:

- the `[[xx]]` **citations** in `todo.md`'s `## Todo` / `## Done` items, and
- the `[xx]:` **definition lines** in `todo.md`'s `# References`,

renumbering both to a compact `[1]..[N]`. The *targets* on the right of each `[xx]:` (e.g. `/notes/chores-09.md#…`, `/ARCHITECTURE.md`, `/notes/forks-multi-user.md`) stay byte-for-byte identical — only the slot number changes.

Nothing else changes:
- **`done.md`** — its own independent file-local numbering; out of scope (so your "just todo.md" answers that question I had).
- **`chores-01..08`** — their `### Edits` lists mention `todo.md` ref-keys in code spans (`` `[86]` ``), but those are *grandfathered historical records* — they describe what was added to `todo.md` *at the time*; renumbering `todo.md` today doesn't rewrite that history, so they stay untouched. (`chores-09` no longer has any such mentions — its `### Edits` lists were removed in `0.48.3`.)
- Nothing else references `todo.md`'s ref-numbers (other files point at `todo.md` by name/section, never by its `[N]` slots).

So: one file changed, `notes/todo.md`. I'd do this as `0.48.4` (`docs: renumber todo.md references (0.48.4)`).

Still need from you: **ordering** — renumber in first-citation-appearance order (proper footnote: `[1]` = ref cited by the first `## Todo` item) or just compact the gaps preserving the current numeric order (`[4]→[1]`, `[5]→[2]`, …)? I lean first-citation order; compact-gaps is simpler.

(And `0.48.3` is still sitting ready for your review-before-commit — let me know when you've looked.)

```json
{
  "file_path": "/tmp/renumber_todo.py",
  "content": "#!/usr/bin/env python3\n\"\"\"Renumber notes/todo.md's references to a compact [1]..[N] in\nfirst-citation-appearance order (scan ## Todo, then ## Done).\nFile-local: only [[N]] citations and [N]: definition lines change;\ntheir targets and every other file are untouched.\n\"\"\"\nimport re\nimport sys\n\nPATH = 'notes/todo.md'\nwith open(PATH) as f:\n    content = f.read()\n\nm = re.search(r'\\n# References\\n', content)\nassert m, \"no # References section found\"\nbody = content[:m.start()]                 # everything before \"\\n# References\\n\"\nrefs_block = content[m.start():]           # \"\\n# References\\n\\n[..]: ...\\n...\"\n\n# Parse ref definitions (only ^[N]: target lines; refs section has no prose)\nref_def_re = re.compile(r'^\\[(\\d+)\\]: (.+)$', re.M)\ndefs = {}\nfor mm in ref_def_re.finditer(refs_block):\n    n = int(mm.group(1))\n    assert n not in defs, f\"duplicate def [{n}]\"\n    defs[n] = mm.group(2)\n\n# Refs block must be exactly \"# References\" + blank + the def lines (nothing else)\nnondef = [ln for ln in refs_block.splitlines()\n          if ln and ln != \"# References\" and not ref_def_re.match(ln)]\nassert not nondef, f\"unexpected non-def lines in # References: {nondef!r}\"\n\n# First-citation order over the body (## Todo then ## Done, in document order)\ncite_re = re.compile(r'\\[\\[(\\d+)\\]\\]')\nseen = []\nfor mm in cite_re.finditer(body):\n    n = int(mm.group(1))\n    if n not in seen:\n        seen.append(n)\n\ncited, defined = set(seen), set(defs)\nif cited != defined:\n    print(f\"MISMATCH cited-only={sorted(cited-defined)} defined-only={sorted(defined-cited)}\")\n    sys.exit(1)\n\nold2new = {old: i + 1 for i, old in enumerate(seen)}\n\nnew_body = cite_re.sub(lambda mm: f'[[{old2new[int(mm.group(1))]}]]', body)\nnew_refs = \"\\n# References\\n\\n\" + \"\\n\".join(\n    f'[{new}]: {defs[old]}' for old, new in sorted(old2new.items(), key=lambda kv: kv[1])\n) + \"\\n\"\n\nwith open(PATH, 'w') as f:\n    f.write(new_body + new_refs)\n\nprint(f\"renumbered {len(seen)} refs (first-citation order)\")\nprint(\"old -> new:\")\nfor old in seen:\n    print(f\"  [{old}] -> [{old2new[old]}]\")\n"
}
```

=== 0.48.3 section in chores-09 ===
## docs: chores-09 → new shape (0.48.3)

Retroactively convert every commit-recording section in
`chores-09.md` to the 0.48.2 convention: section header =
exact commit title; `Commits: [[N]]` first-line citing the
section's commit; `### Edits` subsections removed (the commit
body is the record). Line 114's bare `[76]` (a
foreign-namespace citation attempt) → a `chores-09`-local ref
to the chores-07 `--scope` design section. Lines 38–40's
renumbering description left code-spanned (literal historical
tokens, no stable current target). `notes/README.md` codifies
the footnote framing — ref numbers are file-local slots; a
`[N]` in a code span is a quoted identifier (data), not a
citation; cross-file pointing uses an inline link.

### Why retrofit one file

- **Surfaced by adding `# References`.** 0.48.2 gave
  `chores-09` a `# References` section; a reader then expects
  it to define the page's `[N]` mentions — but most were
  `todo.md`'s / `done.md`'s numbers written into `chores-09`
  prose, a category error. Half-implemented was the worst of
  both; either the page runs the convention fully or it
  doesn't.
- **Bounded.** One file, 21 historical commit-recording
  sections, ~10 back-refs (`todo.md` `[85]`–`[88]`, `done.md`
  `[79]`/`[81]`/`[84]`, two prose mentions in
  `init-clone-refactor-conflict.md`). `chores-01..08` have
  similar vestigials but no `# References` forcing the issue —
  they stay grandfathered (the convention sunsets their
  `### Edits` lists by attrition, not retrofit).

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/wink/data/prgs/rust/vc-x1/notes/chores-09.md",
  "old_string": "  similar vestigials but no `# References` forcing the issue —\n  they stay grandfathered (the convention sunsets their\n  `### Edits` lists by attrition, not retrofit).\n\n# References",
  "new_string": "  similar vestigials but no `# References` forcing the issue —\n  they stay grandfathered (the convention sunsets their\n  `### Edits` lists by attrition, not retrofit).\n\n## docs: renumber todo.md references (0.48.4)\n\nCompact `notes/todo.md`'s `# References` to a contiguous\n`[1]..[N]` in first-citation-appearance order — scan the\n`## Todo` items, then the `## Done` items, in document order,\nand assign `[1]`, `[2]`, … to refs as their first `[[N]]`\ncitation appears. The 0.48.3 retrofits left `todo.md` with a\nsparse namespace (gaps from pruned entries, numbers running\ninto the 90s); the renumber re-packs it so the next new ref\nis `[N+1]`, not `[96]`.\n\n- **File-local.** Only `todo.md`'s `[[N]]` citations and\n  `[N]:` definition lines move; every `[N]:` *target* and\n  every other note file is untouched (ref numbers are\n  file-local slots — `done.md` keeps its own numbering).\n- **First-citation order, not definition order.** The number\n  a reader meets first is `[1]`; following a citation walks\n  *down* the `# References` list, never up. (Definition order\n  would scatter the reading sequence.)\n- **Cosmetic: multi-ref groups sorted ascending.** A citation\n  cluster renders `[[9]],[[13]],[[14]]`, not the order the\n  refs happened to land in.\n\n### Why renumber todo.md only\n\n- `todo.md` is the live churn surface — entries land and get\n  pruned every cycle, so its namespace fragments fastest and\n  benefits most from a re-pack. `chores-NN.md` / `done.md`\n  are append-mostly; their gaps are rare and not worth a\n  rewrite pass.\n- A renumber is a whole-file rewrite of citations + defs;\n  doing it opportunistically (when the namespace has drifted\n  enough to annoy) beats a standing \"keep it dense\" rule that\n  would force a rewrite on every prune.\n\n# References"
}
```

 827  829: 
      830: ## docs: renumber todo.md references (0.48.4)
      831: 
      832: Compact `notes/todo.md`'s `# References` to a contiguous
      833: `[1]..[N]` in first-citation-appearance order — scan the
      834: `## Todo` items, then the `## Done` items, in document order,
      835: and assign `[1]`, `[2]`, … to refs as their first `[[N]]`
      836: citation appears. The 0.48.3 retrofits left `todo.md` with a
      837: sparse namespace (gaps from pruned entries, numbers running
      838: into the 90s); the renumber re-packs it so the next new ref
      839: is `[N+1]`, not `[96]`.
      840: 
      841: - **File-local.** Only `todo.md`'s `[[N]]` citations and
      842:   `[N]:` definition lines move; every `[N]:` *target* and
      843:   every other note file is untouched (ref numbers are
      844:   file-local slots — `done.md` keeps its own numbering).
      845: - **First-citation order, not definition order.** The number
      846:   a reader meets first is `[1]`; following a citation walks
      847:   *down* the `# References` list, never up. (Definition order
      848:   would scatter the reading sequence.)
      849: - **Cosmetic: multi-ref groups sorted ascending.** A citation
      850:   cluster renders `[[9]],[[13]],[[14]]`, not the order the
      851:   refs happened to land in.
      852: 
      853: ### Why renumber todo.md only
      854: 
      855: - `todo.md` is the live churn surface — entries land and get
      856:   pruned every cycle, so its namespace fragments fastest and
      857:   benefits most from a re-pack. `chores-NN.md` / `done.md`
      858:   are append-mostly; their gaps are rare and not worth a
      859:   rewrite pass.
      860: - A renumber is a whole-file rewrite of citations + defs;
      861:   doing it opportunistically (when the namespace has drifted
      862:   enough to annoy) beats a standing "keep it dense" rule that
      863:   would force a rewrite on every prune.

77	## Reference numbering
78	
79	Every note file (`todo.md`, `chores-NN.md`, `done.md`) keeps a
80	file-local `# References` section at the bottom. Reference numbers
81	are scoped to that file — `[1]` in `chores-07.md` and `[1]` in
82	`chores-01.md` are independent slots that may point at completely
83	different URLs. New chores files start their numbering at `[1]`.
84	
85	Treat `[N]` like a **footnote**: the number is a local slot, only
86	meaningful within its file's `# References`. So a `[N]` *citation*
87	(bare `[N]`, or doubled `[[N]]` — see below) never reuses another
88	file's number; to cite a target a sibling file references, pick
89	your own next-local slot and define it (the same target may carry
90	a different number in each file). A `[N]` *inside a code span*
91	(`` `[72]` ``) is different — that's a quoted identifier: literal
92	text naming a ref-key (often from another file's namespace), data,
93	not a citation; it needs no definition here. To point at a section
94	of another file from prose, use an inline link with an anchor —
95	`[that section](../chores-07.md#…)` — not a bare number.
96	
97	A `chores-NN.md` `# References` entry is usually a
98	`/notes/<file>.md#anchor` (or `/ARCHITECTURE.md`) path, but may
99	also be a **commit reference** —
100	`[N]: <commit-url-with-12-hex-SHA> "<full-40-hex-SHA>"`, cited by
101	a section's `Commits:` line. See
102	[`../CLAUDE.md`](../CLAUDE.md#chores-commit-references) for the
103	why and the exact shape.
104	
105	## Retiring Done entries
106	
107	`todo.md`'s `## Done` section is a rolling buffer of recently shipped
108	work, not a permanent log. Move entries into `done.md` at two natural
109	beats:
110	
111	- **Closing a ladder** — when the final `X.Y.Z` (no suffix) commit
```

> DEVELOPER

Just curios why the first entry of chores-09 is [2] and [1] is almost at the bottom?

> AGENT

Because chores-09's `# References` was numbered in *the order the slots got assigned as we did the conversion work* — not document order, and not first-citation order.

The timeline:

- **0.48.2** created the *first* new-shape section, `## docs: por-dual capture + icr cleanup (0.48.1)`. Its `Commits:` ref got the first free slot → `[1]`. That section is near the bottom of the file because chores files grow newest-at-bottom.
- **0.48.3** then did the bulk retrofit of the 21 *historical* commit-recording sections, top-to-bottom, handing them `[2]` … `[22]` — so `[2]` is the oldest section (`chore: init-clone-refactor rebase landing (0.42.0-4.7)`), right at the top.
- Then `[23]` → the 0.48.2 section, `[24]` → the chores-07 design ref (the line-114 fix), and now `[25]` → the 0.48.3 section's commit.

So `[1]` is "first slot allocated," which happened to be a recent section. It's exactly the namespace-fragmentation the README paragraph I just added describes — and that paragraph currently says chores files *aren't* re-packed. chores-09 is the odd one out: it just got a one-time retrofit, so its numbering is scrambled in a way append-only growth wouldn't produce.

I can fix it — re-pack chores-09's `# References` to `[1]..[25]` in first-citation (≈ document) order, same file-local rewrite as todo.md. Two ways to sequence it:

- **Fold into 0.48.4** — it's the same kind of change, keeps the "renumber" cycle's scope coherent.
- **Separate `0.48.5` micro-cycle** — keeps 0.48.4 strictly "todo.md only" as planned, and the README note that chores files *can* be re-packed would then have a worked example.

Which do you want? (Or leave chores-09 as-is — the slots resolve fine, it's just not in reading order.)

> AGENT

Yes — that's exactly the approach, and it's the same shape the `todo.md` script used:

- **array (1)** = old-ref-numbers in first-citation order → its index `i` gives the new number `i+1` (`old2new = {old: i+1}`).
- **array (2)** = `old-number → ref-string` (the `[N]:` target).
- New `# References` = for each new slot in order, take `old = array1[slot]`, emit `array2[old]`.

One wrinkle specific to chores-09: the `## docs: renumber todo.md references (0.48.4)` section I just added has a *code-spanned* example — `` `[[9]],[[13]],[[14]]` `` — which is a literal illustration, not a citation (per the README footnote framing: a `[[N]]` inside a code span is a quoted token, data). So the script masks fenced ```` ``` ```` blocks and inline `` `…` `` spans first, scans/rewrites only the prose, then stitches the code segments back verbatim. Net effect on chores-09: `[2]…[22]` shift down to `[1]…[21]`, `[24]` (the chores-07 design ref, first cited up at line 108) becomes `[3]`, and the recent `[1]/[23]/[25]` sections (0.48.1/0.48.2/0.48.3) move to `[23]/[24]/[25]`.

```json
{
  "file_path": "/tmp/renumber_chores09.py",
  "content": "#!/usr/bin/env python3\n\"\"\"Renumber notes/chores-09.md's references to a compact [1]..[N] in\nfirst-citation-appearance order (document order). File-local: only\n[[N]] citations and [N]: definition lines change; their targets and\nevery other file are untouched. Code spans (fenced ``` blocks and\ninline `...` spans) are masked first, so a code-spanned `[[N]]` is a\nliteral token, not a citation (per notes/README.md footnote framing).\n\"\"\"\nimport re\nimport sys\n\nPATH = 'notes/chores-09.md'\nwith open(PATH) as f:\n    content = f.read()\n\nm = re.search(r'\\n# References\\n', content)\nassert m, \"no # References section found\"\nbody = content[:m.start()]\nrefs_block = content[m.start():]\n\n# Parse ref definitions (refs section is def lines only, no prose)\nref_def_re = re.compile(r'^\\[(\\d+)\\]: (.+)$', re.M)\ndefs = {}\nfor mm in ref_def_re.finditer(refs_block):\n    n = int(mm.group(1))\n    assert n not in defs, f\"duplicate def [{n}]\"\n    defs[n] = mm.group(2)\n\nnondef = [ln for ln in refs_block.splitlines()\n          if ln and ln != \"# References\" and not ref_def_re.match(ln)]\nassert not nondef, f\"unexpected non-def lines in # References: {nondef!r}\"\n\n# Split body into alternating prose / code segments. Code = fenced\n# ```...``` blocks or inline `...` spans (verbatim, never rewritten);\n# only the even-indexed prose segments are scanned and rewritten.\nCODE = re.compile(r'(```.*?```|`[^`\\n]*`)', re.S)\nsegs = CODE.split(body)\n\ncite_re = re.compile(r'\\[\\[(\\d+)\\]\\]')\n\n# First-citation order over prose segments, in document order\nseen = []\nfor i in range(0, len(segs), 2):\n    for mm in cite_re.finditer(segs[i]):\n        n = int(mm.group(1))\n        if n not in seen:\n            seen.append(n)\n\ncited, defined = set(seen), set(defs)\nif cited != defined:\n    print(f\"MISMATCH cited-only={sorted(cited-defined)} defined-only={sorted(defined-cited)}\")\n    sys.exit(1)\n\nold2new = {old: i + 1 for i, old in enumerate(seen)}\n\n\ndef rewrite(prose):\n    out = cite_re.sub(lambda mm: f'[[{old2new[int(mm.group(1))]}]]', prose)\n    # Cosmetic: sort multi-ref citation groups ascending\n    out = re.sub(\n        r'\\[\\[\\d+\\]\\](?:,\\[\\[\\d+\\]\\])+',\n        lambda mm: \",\".join(f'[[{n}]]' for n in sorted(int(x) for x in re.findall(r'\\d+', mm.group(0)))),\n        out,\n    )\n    return out\n\n\nfor i in range(0, len(segs), 2):\n    segs[i] = rewrite(segs[i])\n\nnew_body = \"\".join(segs)\n\nnew_refs = \"\\n# References\\n\\n\" + \"\\n\".join(\n    f'[{new}]: {defs[old]}' for old, new in sorted(old2new.items(), key=lambda kv: kv[1])\n) + \"\\n\"\n\nwith open(PATH, 'w') as f:\n    f.write(new_body + new_refs)\n\nprint(f\"renumbered {len(seen)} refs (first-citation order)\")\nprint(\"old -> new:\")\nfor old in seen:\n    print(f\"  [{old}] -> [{old2new[old]}]\")\n"
}
```
